People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… maybe sometimes, but I don’t really notice that one as much. Usually I’m more aware when I can’t stay on something.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… from what my mom says, it was there pretty early. Grade school kind of stuff — out of my seat, blurting answers, losing homework. I’d get notes sent home, even though I guess I could do the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really strong swings, I guess. I can get irritated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed big mood changes like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these symptoms at home or when you're not at work, like when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not just work. It’s… not great.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. I’ll finish an incident report thinking it’s fine, and then the captain catches stuff — wrong unit number, a missing time stamp, some box I skipped that actually matters for the safety review. Stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… maybe sometimes, but I don’t really notice that one as much. Usually I’m more aware when I can’t stay on something.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… from what my mom says, it was there pretty early. Grade school kind of stuff — out of my seat, blurting answers, losing homework. I’d get notes sent home, even though I guess I could do the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really strong swings, I guess. I can get irritated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed big mood changes like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these symptoms at home or when you're not at work, like when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not just work. At home too. Like on days off, if we’re supposed to just sit and do something quiet with the family, I don’t last long. I’ll start talking, or I’m up moving around, checking something in the garage… tools or whatever. I just don’t stay sitting very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overly angry or frustrated for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: Not really for no reason, no. I mean, I get frustrated sometimes, but there’s usually something setting it off. I wouldn’t call it out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are causing problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… maybe sometimes it annoys people, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s causing big relationship problems. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. My mom’s said I was like this even back in grade school. Getting up out of my seat, blurting stuff out, losing homework… notes getting sent home. She always says I was smart enough to do it, I just… didn’t keep it together.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been in a situation where your behavior or symptoms got you into trouble or caused significant problems at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at work, mostly. It’s gotten to the point where the guys don’t really trust me with details like they used to. I got pulled into a performance and safety meeting over it, which was… not great.\n\nI was hoping to move into more of a leadership role, but now it feels like that’s slipping away because the crew can’t count on me to keep track of things consistently.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with paying attention or your restlessness and fidgeting causes more problems for you?\n\nPatient: Probably the restlessness… the always being keyed up. I feel like I’m running hot most of the time, like I can’t really shut the motor off. That’s more of what’s been getting me into trouble at the firehouse lately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I feel like I’m always running hot. Like I can’t shut the motor off. And lately it’s been causing problems at the firehouse.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… if I’m sitting in a meeting, my leg’s usually bouncing. I’ll click a pen, shift around, mess with whatever’s on the table. Half the time I don’t even realize I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Mostly at work. Like when the guys are talking through a plan, I’ll jump in because I already think I know the answer. Then I realize I cut somebody off or kind of messed up the flow. It’s… not great.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. I’ll finish an incident report thinking it’s fine, and then the captain catches stuff — wrong unit number, a missing time stamp, some box I skipped that actually matters for the safety review. Stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… maybe sometimes, but I don’t really notice that one as much. Usually I’m more aware when I can’t stay on something.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… from what my mom says, it was there pretty early. Grade school kind of stuff — out of my seat, blurting answers, losing homework. I’d get notes sent home, even though I guess I could do the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really strong swings, I guess. I can get irritated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed big mood changes like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these symptoms at home or when you're not at work, like when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not just work. At home too. Like on days off, if we’re supposed to just sit and do something quiet with the family, I don’t last long. I’ll start talking, or I’m up moving around, checking something in the garage… tools or whatever. I just don’t stay sitting very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overly angry or frustrated for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: Not really for no reason, no. I mean, I get frustrated sometimes, but there’s usually something setting it off. I wouldn’t call it out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are causing problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… maybe sometimes it annoys people, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s causing big relationship problems. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. My mom’s said I was like this even back in grade school. Getting up out of my seat, blurting stuff out, losing homework… notes getting sent home. She always says I was smart enough to do it, I just… didn’t keep it together.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been in a situation where your behavior or symptoms got you into trouble or caused significant problems at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at work, mostly. It’s gotten to the point where the guys don’t really trust me with details like they used to. I got pulled into a performance and safety meeting over it, which was… not great.\n\nI was hoping to move into more of a leadership role, but now it feels like that’s slipping away because the crew can’t count on me to keep track of things consistently.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with paying attention or your restlessness and fidgeting causes more problems for you?\n\nPatient: Probably the restlessness… the always being keyed up. I feel like I’m running hot most of the time, like I can’t really shut the motor off. That’s more of what’s been getting me into trouble at the firehouse lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with paying attention or with controlling your impulses and activity level?\n\nPatient: More the impulse and activity thing, I guess. I’m just… always running hot, like I can’t shut the motor off. That’s the part that’s been getting me in trouble at the firehouse lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with paying attention or your impulsivity and hyperactivity causes more problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more the hyperactive side, I think. I just feel like I’m always running hot, like I can’t shut the motor off. That’s what’s been causing problems at the firehouse lately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like when the guys are talking through a plan, I’ll jump in because I already think I know the answer. Then I realize I cut somebody off or kind of messed up the flow. It’s… not great.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. I’ll finish an incident report thinking it’s fine, and then the captain catches stuff — wrong unit number, a missing time stamp, some box I skipped that actually matters for the safety review. Stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… maybe sometimes, but I don’t really notice that one as much. Usually I’m more aware when I can’t stay on something.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… from what my mom says, it was there pretty early. Grade school kind of stuff — out of my seat, blurting answers, losing homework. I’d get notes sent home, even though I guess I could do the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really strong swings, I guess. I can get irritated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed big mood changes like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these symptoms at home or when you're not at work, like when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not just work. At home too. Like on days off, if we’re supposed to just sit and do something quiet with the family, I don’t last long. I’ll start talking, or I’m up moving around, checking something in the garage… tools or whatever. I just don’t stay sitting very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overly angry or frustrated for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: Not really for no reason, no. I mean, I get frustrated sometimes, but there’s usually something setting it off. I wouldn’t call it out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are causing problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… maybe sometimes it annoys people, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s causing big relationship problems. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. My mom’s said I was like this even back in grade school. Getting up out of my seat, blurting stuff out, losing homework… notes getting sent home. She always says I was smart enough to do it, I just… didn’t keep it together.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been in a situation where your behavior or symptoms got you into trouble or caused significant problems at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at work, mostly. I feel like I’m running hot most of the time, like I can’t really shut the motor off. That’s more of what’s been getting me into trouble at the firehouse lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with paying attention or with controlling your impulses and activity level?\n\nPatient: More the impulse and activity thing, I guess. I’m just… always running hot, like I can’t shut the motor off. That’s the part that’s been getting me in trouble at the firehouse lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with paying attention or your impulsivity and hyperactivity causes more problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more the hyperactive side, I think. I just feel like I’m always running hot, like I can’t shut the motor off. That’s what’s been causing problems at the firehouse lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where these symptoms seemed to disappear or significantly decrease?\n\nPatient: Not really… not in the last year or so. It’s been a real problem for, I don’t know, about a year and five months—since the longer rotating shifts started, and they added all the extra documentation and drone stuff. Before that it was there, but not like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your problems with paying attention or your problems with restlessness and impulsivity cause you more trouble overall?\n\nPatient: Probably the restlessness and impulse side. That’s what’s been getting me in trouble at the firehouse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your problems with paying attention or your problems with restlessness and impulsivity are about equally troublesome for you?\n\nPatient: Not really equally, no.
